3	
4	Some of the abstract language here definitely matches up: the author did draw a conclusion about a population (the 5,000 recipients) based on observations of a subgroup (the 300 respondents).
5	
6	Can we say that the sample of respondents is quite unlikely to be unrepresentative of the overall population of recipients?
7	
8	That seems to be overdoing it a little, but this is our best available answer. Does LSAC just think that "respondents" on any mailed out survey are an inherently unlikely sample?
9	
10	Yes, apparently, and probably with good reason. As we covered before, it''s a well documented phenomenon with product reviews / teacher evaluations and such that the people most likely to respond are the ones with the most extreme opinions.
11	
12	We can also enlist our common sense -- if you got a mailer from a nonprofit you didn''t agree with, would you fill out their survey and mail it back?
13	
14	No, of course not. Why would you waste your time helping out this organization whose cause you don''t even support? 128	('39f3d312-56b6-4593-8324-01938f1f5786'::uuid,'"Offers no evidence" is an uncommon version of "takes for granted / presumes / fails to establish". All of these just mean, the author is assuming X.
129	
130	So is this author assuming that the people who were surveyed would have responded differently had they been asked the same questions in years prior?
131	
132	Yes! His hypothesis commits him to that assumption. If he thinks that as we get older we get less willing to reveal personal finances, then we would assume that had these older people been asked the same survey questions two or three decades prior, they would have been less cagey, less aloof, more willing to answer the questions.
133	
134	If we negated this assumption, it would turn into a big objection: yo, author -- these people would have answered the same way if they had been asked this as a younger version of themselves.
135	
136	In other words, it''s not getting older that is making them unwilling to talk about money; they''ve always been like that. Maybe the culture of Baby Boomers growing up was to never talk about money, so Baby Boomers when they were 20, 30, 40, 50, etc. have always been reluctant to discuss their personal earnings. Thus, the older people in this survey (Boomers) were unwilling to talk money.
137	
138	Meanwhile, Millennials and Gen Z were raised to talk about how unfair it is that they make crappy wages while their corporate overlords plunder a planet in its final throes of civilization before climate change turns us all into water-pirates. So they are happy to talk about money now, and they will continue to be when they''re 40, 50, 60. etc. Thus, the younger people in this survey (Millennials and Gen Z) are willing to talk money.','"Offers no evidence" is an uncommon version of "takes for granted / presumes / fails to establish". 149	('bea6df75-a580-4720-9316-b6a5cbb05d09'::uuid,'This is what we predicted. The idea behind Inference questions is testing our ability to synthesize. 280	('6994ff45-fcfe-40bf-9598-0b1aa855705b'::uuid,'If we switched "political leaders" for "authors of the constitution", then this answer would be pretty close to reflecting the conversation.
281	
282	Today''s political leaders have to pretend like their interpretations of the constitution express the intentions of the authors of the constitution, or else we don''t feel like laws are the bequest of a long tradition, and thus we lose political stability.
283	
284	If we think laws are just the preferences of contemporary politicians (i.e. political leaders), the necessary fiction "would vanish".','If we switched "political leaders" for "authors of the constitution", then this answer would be pretty close to reflecting the conversation.
285	
286	Today''s political leaders have to pretend like their interpretations of the constitution express the intentions of the authors of the constitution, or else we don''t feel like laws are the bequest of a long tradition, and thus we lose political stability.
287	
288	If we think laws are just the preferences of contemporary politicians (i.e. political leaders), the necessary fiction "would vanish".
289	
290	So (A) gets the danger backwards. Stability is threatened when people come to believe the laws express what today''s leaders want. People not believing that is exactly what the fiction protects, so nothing here suggests it would make the nation less stable.'),
291	('130091fc-91ca-44dc-a905-f204707fa54a'::uuid,'This echoes the chain we put together:
292	
293	If they didn''t we''d lose illusion lose make it sound → that laws are → political that (fictional) way part of tradition stability
294	
295	What is "this fictional way", that if it ceased would lead to the loss of our illusion and the loss of political stability?
296	
297	It''s what''s covered in the 2nd sentence -- we portray our modern interpretations of the constitution as though they embody the intentions of its authors.
298	
299	If we ceased perpetuating this fiction, and then people stopped thinking of the actions of contemporary politicians as though those actions embodied the authors'' intentions, then we would lose the illusion of a long standing legal tradition and thus lose political stability.

312	('50826210-3e6f-486c-954e-6397a903c023'::uuid,'This answer does a little word salad with the three concepts in our conditional chain. Here is what we were able to derive:
313	
314	If they didn''t we''d lose illusion lose make it sound → that laws are → political that (fictional) way part of tradition stability A → B → C
315	
316	And this answer is saying that B is what causes A to require C.
317	
318	That''s actually kind of tempting, because in a conditional sense, B is what links A to C and thus B is what makes C a necessary implication of A. But the problem is the causal verb "makes", not the conditional logic. Suppose we said this: Getting into Harvard requires a good LSAT score, which requires that you can read/write in English.
319	
320	Is it fair to derive from that "The need for a good LSAT score makes getting into Harvard dependent on being able to read/write in English"?
321	
322	No. That need may exist independently of needing a good LSAT score. After all, getting into Harvard requires submitting personal statements, which requires knowing how to read/write in English. Even though 3 things are all connected in a chain of requirements, we can''t automatically say that the second thing is what causes there to be a dependent relationship between the 1st and 3rd.','This answer does a little word salad with the three concepts in our conditional chain. Here is what we were able to derive:

333

335	
336	No. That need may exist independently of needing a good LSAT score. After all, getting into Harvard requires submitting personal statements, which requires knowing how to read/write in English. Even though 3 things are all connected in a chain of requirements, we can''t automatically say that the second thing is what causes there to be a dependent relationship between the 1st and 3rd.'),
337	('056c0c48-b0d0-485f-a03d-d7afa342f415'::uuid,'At first we might think, "This doesn''t have to be true. Yes, we know that most mail takes 3 or more days, but that doesn''t have to be because it''s incorrectly addressed. It could be that it''s correctly addressed but damaged in transit."
338	
339	Here''s the problem with that -- if we tell ourselves that "most correctly addressed mail is damaged in transit, and thus takes more than 2 days", then we''d be contradicting the first sentence, which says that "nearly all correctly addressed mail arrives within 2 days".
340	
341	If all mail were correctly addressed, then this paragraph would contradict itself.
342	
343	If there were 100 pieces of mail, all correctly addressed, we know that nearly all (we''ll say 90 of them) get there within 2 days. That leaves 10 pieces of correctly addressed mail not getting there within 2 days.
344	
345	But we were told that "most pieces of mail" take more than 2 days. 10 is not most of 100. We''d have to keep adding incorrectly addressed mail in order to be able to every honor the idea that most total mail takes more than 2 days.
346	
347	? pieces of mail 100 correctly addressed ? incorrect 90 arrive w/in 2 days 10 take more than 2 (damaged in transit)
348	
349	Let''s say we added in 50 incorrectly addressed pieces of mail.
350	
351	150pieces of mail 100 correctly addressed50incorrect 90 arrive w/in 2 days 10 take more than 2 (damaged in transit)
352	
353	Even if all 50 of those took more than 2 days, and we added in the 10 correctly addressed ones that took more than 2 days, we''d still only be at 60 pieces that took more than 2 days.
354	
355	And 60 isnotmost of 150. It''s less than half. So we''d have to add even more incorrectly addressed pieces of mail.
356	
357	180pieces of mail 100 correctly addressed80incorrect 90 arrive w/in 2 days 10 take more than 2 (damaged in transit)
358	
359	Now we could have a total of 90 pieces of mail that took more than 2 days (80 + 10).
360	
361	90 out of 180 is exactly half, so we''d still need to go a little higher with our incorrectly addressed total to make the "most" fact work.
362	
363	No matter what number of mail pieces we make the correctly addressed mail, we''ll always have the problem that 90% of it gets there within 2 days and only 10% or less takes more than 2 days.
364	
365	In order to comply with the fact that more than 50% of all mail takes more than 2 days, we need to add a lot of incorrectly addressed mail to make the math work.
366	
367	"Large proportion" doesn''t have a specific numerical cut-off, but it looks like we''ll always need more than 40% of the mail to be incorrectly addressed in order to make all this math work, and more than 40% is definitely "a large proportion".','At first we might think, "This doesn''t have to be true. Yes, we know that most mail takes 3 or more days, but that doesn''t have to be because it''s incorrectly addressed. It could be that it''s correctly addressed but damaged in transit."

376

378	- 100 correctly addressed
379	  - 90 arrive w/in 2 days
380	  - 10 take more than 2 (damaged in transit)
381	- ? incorrect

384

386	- 100 correctly addressed
387	  - 90 arrive w/in 2 days
388	  - 10 take more than 2 (damaged in transit)
389	- 50 incorrect
390	
391	Even if all 50 of those took more than 2 days, and we added in the 10 correctly addressed ones that took more than 2 days, we''d still only be at 60 pieces that took more than 2 days.

394	
395	180 pieces of mail

399	- 80 incorrect

401	Now we could have a total of 90 pieces of mail that took more than 2 days (80 + 10).
402	
403	90 out of 180 is exactly half, so we''d still need to go a little higher with our incorrectly addressed total to make the "most" fact work.

410	('8ac791d6-40a3-4b8c-ae64-8a698bece2a0'::uuid,'We couldn''t prove that there was any damaged mail that was correctly addressed. 432	('89a7d408-8842-47f6-99b2-52622fb4ce7b'::uuid,'Let''s stop at the trigger and ask ourselves if we know anything ...
433	were there any people that always guessed the next image would appear at the top?
434	
435	We don''t really know. We know that most of them didn''t guess that, since they were wrong most of the time. And the final sentence is giving us a counterfactual about what would have been true if they had all always guessed "next image will be on top".
436	
437	So given that we don''t know anything about this trigger, it''s hard to say we can derive a must be true of what would follow.
438	
439	If we assume that nobody was constantly guessing "next image on top", then this answer is just talking about a counterfactual.
440	
441	It''s saying, "Yeah, nobody guessed X, but if they had guessed X, it wouldn''t have been based on any pattern they believed they saw."
442	
443	How would we prove that counterfactual?
444	
445	How can we say in a hypothetical situation what someone would / wouldn''t have believed they saw in the sequence?
446	
447	The contrapositive of this conditional rule is saying:
448	```
449	if they were basing their guesses on a pattern they believed they saw
450	  → they would not have always guessed next image on top
451	```
452	We know for a fact that all of the subjects were guessing based on a pattern they believed they saw.
453	
454	So according to this answer choice, none of them would have guessed "next image will appear on top".
455	
456	That''s too strong. We know most of them weren''t guessing "next image will appear on top" because most of the time they were wrong, but it''s still possible that some of the subjects guessed "next image will appear on top".
457	
458	It''s possible that some subjects guessed perfectly. The low accuracy of all the other subjects could lower the group''s accuracy to where it''s still true to say "the subjects guessed correctly less than half of the time".
459	
460	(as a desperation move, you might avoid guessing this tempting answer because Must Be True questions often put a very tempting but wrong answer non-coincidentally into the slot for choice (A). 502	('76529ac9-1ffb-4f3a-8a3b-ad7d352d58d2'::uuid,'This is yet another variation of what (A) and (C) did. In fact, this answer is so equivalent to the meaning of (C), that it would prevent either one of them from being correct (there can''t be two correct answers).
503	
504	The government''s reason for limiting the nuclear industry''s liability was that "unlimited liability poses a threat to the financial security of the nuclear industry".
505	
506	According to this answer, that claim was false. So therefore the government misrepresented their reason (or they were confused about whether liability really does present a threat).
507	
508	In any case, (A), (C), and (D) are taking the conflict we have between "no reason to fear an accident" and "there is reason to take steps to avoid financial calamity in the case of an accident", and arbitrarily deciding which of those conflicting ideas is the true one and which is the false one.','This is yet another variation of what (A) and (C) did. 515	('20b539ef-14c0-4617-b2f8-6f6eb4e58b79'::uuid,'This is talking about "Most late career works". We don''t have any statements about most-late career, so it''s not going to be a correct answer.

245	('ae832ee9-55e5-4ab0-a596-0ffc5d60d76a'::uuid,'Yes, "Gillette''s argument is unconvincing" is a perfect meaning match for "Gillette''s argument is not persuasive".

338	('7820d154-bf26-458f-bb4f-7f046e03f77d'::uuid,'This is not really anything we''d ever see. A logical consequence of a claim or a plan is something we''d sometimes see.

341	
342	A consequence / implication / inference of an argument would probably mean "something that can be inferred from the conclusion, assuming it''s true".

344	But when have you ever seen an argument on LSAT present evidence, present a conclusion, and thenalsopresent a further logical implication of that conclusion?
345	
346	If they did that, then we would just be calling the first conclusion an Intermediate Conclusion and the consequence of that conclusion would be the Main Conclusion.
347	
348	In short, this answer is gibberish.','This is not really anything we''d ever see. A logical consequence of a claim or a plan is something we''d sometimes see.
349	
350	For example, if I think that "Michael Jordan, who played in the 20th century, is the best basketball player ever", then a consequence of that view is that "the best basketball player did not play in the 19th century".
351	
352	A consequence / implication / inference of an argument would probably mean "something that can be inferred from the conclusion, assuming it''s true".
353	
354	But when have you ever seen an argument on LSAT present evidence, present a conclusion, and then also present a further logical implication of that conclusion?

A logical consequence of a claim or a plan is something we''d sometimes see.\n\nFor example, if I think that \"Michael Jordan, who played in the 20th century, is the best basketball player ever\", then a consequence of that view is that \"the best basketball player did not play in the 19th century\".\n\nA consequence / implication / inference of an argument would probably mean \"something that can be inferred from the conclusion, assuming it''s true\".\n\nBut when have you ever seen an argument on LSAT present evidence, present a conclusion, and thenalsopresent a further logical implication of that conclusion?\n\nIf they did that, then we would just be calling the first conclusion an Intermediate Conclusion and the consequence of that conclusion would be the Main Conclusion.\n\nIn short, this answer is gibberish.','This is not really anything we''d ever see. A logical consequence of a claim or a plan is something we''d sometimes see.\n\nFor example, if I think that \"Michael Jordan, who played in the 20th century, is the best basketball player ever\", then a consequence of that view is that \"the best basketball player did not play in the 19th century\".\n\nA consequence / implication / inference of an argument would probably mean \"something that can be inferred from the conclusion, assuming it''s true\".\n\nBut when have you ever seen an argument on LSAT present evidence, present a conclusion, and then also present a further logical implication of that conclusion?\n\nIf they did that, then we would just be calling the first conclusion an Intermediate Conclusion and the consequence of that conclusion would be the Main Conclusion.\n\nIn short, this answer is gibberish.'),\n('c5a7b386-36b2-431f-b2e2-ca9b13899172'::uuid,'This paragraph had, like, nothing to do with the unstated assumption they''re asking us about, since our paragraph is about people whodidn''ttake penicillin, and the claim they''re asking us is about people whodotake it.\n\nThis paragraph was definitely notconclusively provinganything about some people who do take penicillin.','This paragraph had, like, nothing to do with the unstated assumption they''re asking us about, since our paragraph is about people who didn''t take penicillin, and the claim they''re asking us is about people who do take it.\n\nThis paragraph was definitely not conclusively proving anything about some people who do take penicillin.'),\n('22b214ee-0484-4292-88f7-cf20a47a566d'::uuid,'This is saying that \"if people whodotake penicillin develop resistant-bacteria, that rules out the possibility that people whodon''ttake penicillin develop resistant-bacteria.\"\n\nHuh? 44	('3f4a6de3-bc52-4280-a964-20d222ec1459'::uuid,'Saying “to the extent that ____ “ definitely sounds like you''re limiting the application of something.

46	“To the extent that Gilly''s Games Guide will fully prep you for the Games section, it''s a great LSAT resource, but it won''t help much with LR or RC.” I limited the application of GGG''s usefulness to the games section.

48	“You can''t blame the profit motive for homelessness” would apply the argument to the whole problem of homelessness.

88	('be1cd37b-da70-4d59-a63a-fcd2dc6b2447'::uuid,'This answer is terribly written, but it wins by the standard of best available. It''s certainly similar in sentiment to what we were looking for, "the Nobel rules unfairly exclude some deserving people from getting a Nobel".

184	('4a86f571-3d74-410f-ae3c-a2122f31efe1'::uuid,'In 99% of cases, the word “most” is wrong on Necessary Assumption. But in this case, the idea of “a majority / most people” is logically important because the conclusion is about “most people who commit embezzlement / bribery”.
185	In fact, we could even have thought to ourselves, “There’s a New Term in the conclusion! It’s talking about most people who commit embezzlement / bribery, but the evidence never defines or discusses that group. Therefore, this new term should be in my correct answer.”
186	If we negate this, it’s saying, “Most people who commit these two crimes do not do so repeatedly.” Why would that weaken?
187	Because … the trigger that starts this whole causal chain is motion is the idea in the first sentence that if you repeatedly commit these crimes and don’t get caught, you’ll end up being more confident, which will lead to commit these crimes again, which will keep increasing the odds that you eventually get caught.
188	But if most people who commit these two crimes only do so once, then the author doesn’t get that causal chain he’s envisioning, in which the repeat offender keeps increasing the odds of being caught. If most people who commit these crimes do so only once, then they can just commit the crime, not get caught, and be done. Thus, they are not likely to eventually get caught.','In 99% of cases, the word “most” is wrong on Necessary Assumption. 193	('f10c6c6c-1d48-4f04-a777-389143732997'::uuid,'"The only" is strong, and thus a red flag, but also conditional, and thus something we should examine by looking at it conditionally (as well as its contrapositive).
194	
195	"The only" is a sufficient, Left-side indicator word.
196	(only and only if are necessary, Right-side indicator words)
197	
198	So the idea following "the only", a means of making itself unpalatable to predators, would go on the Left of the arrow.
199	
200	```
201	a way to make itself      feeding on
202	highly unpalatable    →   the Natal
203	to predators              grass cycad
204	```
205	
206	When correct answers are conditional, they''re usually written in contrapositive form (to be sneaky, to disguise their potential appeal). When we contrapose this one, we get:
207	
208	```
209	can''t feed on the        no means to make
210	Natal grass cycad    →   itself highly yucky
211	                         to predators
212	```
213	
214	Did the author''s argument make that move?
215	
216	Sure. Not explicitly, but that''s definitely the intended meaning of how the author goes from thinking, "Oh, no, the plant that gives them their anti-predator toxin is endangered. Thus they are endangered."
217	
218	He must be thinking that without this plant, the moth loses this defense mechanism, and thus the predators will be able to gobble them up to the point where their species will be in danger of surviving.

220	This answer choice is just calling out that first thought, that "without this plant, the moth will lose the defense mechanism of making itself taste yucky."

222	If we negate this answer and say, "the cycad isn''t the only way the moth can make itself unpalatable. 245	Sure. Not explicitly, but that''s definitely the intended meaning of how the author goes from thinking, "Oh, no, the plant that gives them their anti-predator toxin is endangered. Thus they are endangered."

There is at least one other way to make itself unpalatable", then that would badly weaken the argument, since it would make it seem like the moths aren''t so vulnerable to predators all of a sudden.'),
252	('42674041-f230-4041-bd26-dff1ada92f6a'::uuid,'This has the lovable ruling-out language "cannot", no let''s negate it and see if it weakens:

265	('feb095d8-cbcb-48de-8086-e81a4521be2d'::uuid,'If we ever see the Conclusion as part of the trigger, then the answer is automatically wrong.
266	
267	For example, if an answer said,
268	(A) If the public is unafraid of scientists, then ...
269	
270	We''d immediately eliminate it. The conclusion is supposed to be on the right side (the outcome) of a conditional reasoning move.
271	
272	We want to see
273	premise → conclusion
274	
275	However, when you contrapose that reasoning move (as so many correct answers do), you get:
276	not conclusion → not premise
277	
278	So seeing the negation of the conclusion in the trigger is a great sign. That''s what we''re seeing here. The answer says,
279	
280	```
281	if public is afraid          over several decades
282	of scientists          →     a scientist would meet
283	                             someone afraid of sci''s
284	```
285	
286	And the contrapositive of this looks just like the Premise → Conclusion move of the argument!
287	
288	```
289	If over several decades       public is not
290	a scientist has not met   →   afraid of
291	someone afraid of sci''s       scientists
292	```','If we see the Conclusion itself as the trigger, the answer is almost certainly wrong.

320	('a0a73a22-677b-4e88-91fe-f5baeb285b9a'::uuid,'If we negate this "not / no" and get, "babies do pay more attention to phrases with simple structures", does that weaken the argument?
321	
322	Not in any clear way. This is talking about paying more attention not being helped extra when it comes to learning the language.
323	
324	And the answer is just talking about any simply-structured phrase, not repeating simple phrases. Our author might agree that it''s better to speak to babies with simple structures because they pay more attention, but still say that repeating these simple phrases has no learning value.','If we negate this "not / no" and get, "babies do pay more attention to phrases with simple structures", does that weaken the argument?

328	And the answer is just talking about any simply-structured phrase, not repeating simple phrases. Our author might agree that it''s better to speak to babies with simple structures because they pay more attention, but still say that repeating these simple phrases has no learning value.'),
329	('c405bcc2-0c2a-40f1-a080-33924a54fbd7'::uuid,'This has the lovable quality of ruling-out with a "not / no", which is so common among correct answers on Necessary Assumption.
330	
331	When we see these ruled-out ideas, we should use the Negation test. If we remove the "not / no", does this answer turn into an objection?
332	
333	The telecom equip imported into Swould beavailable solely to top government officials.
334	
335	Heck yeah that''s an objection. The manufacturer is suggesting that by exporting phones and faxes, we can get to where telecom equipment is widely available to the population of S, at which point repression is impossible.
336	
337	But when we negate this answer, it''s saying, "Yo, manufacturers -- what if the leaders of S don''t allow these exports to be disseminated widely among the population. If the leaders keep all the phones and faxes exclusively to themselves, then we''ve basically just given them a gift when we''re supposed to be isolating them and depriving them of things."
338	
339	Another way to think about this answer is that the manufacturers were clearly thinking that by exporting phones and faxes, the result would be that telecom equipment is widely available to the people of S, and in order for that to be true, it would have to be the case that the government doesn''t keep all that imported telecom equipment exclusively for itself.','This has the lovable quality of ruling-out with a "not / no", which is so common among correct answers on Necessary Assumption.

If the leaders keep all the phones and faxes exclusively to themselves, then we''ve basically just given them a gift when we''re supposed to be isolating them and depriving them of things."

350	('dcd39393-f9bd-4f1c-9f28-98921726bc55'::uuid,'The word/concept of "most" is wrong on Necessary Assumption, 98% of the time we see it, since the difference between most and not-most is a usually-meaningless move from at least 51% to at most 49%.

352	Furthermore, if we negate this answer, it would strengthen, which is not how a correct answer would behave. The negation says that a majority of legislatorsdofavor exempting telecom equipment, so a majority of legislators agree with the conclusion.

354	The negation of the correct answer should weaken, not strengthen.','The word/concept of "most" is wrong on Necessary Assumption, 98% of the time we see it, since the difference between most and not-most is a usually-meaningless move from at least 51% to at most 49%.
355	
356	Furthermore, if we negate this answer, it would strengthen, which is not how a correct answer would behave. The negation says that a majority of legislators do favor exempting telecom equipment, so a majority of legislators agree with the conclusion.
357	
358	The negation of the correct answer should weaken, not strengthen.'),
359	('e724ea31-5bed-4781-928c-3adf29d8ae3d'::uuid,'We don''t have any language to support this superlative claim that telecom equipment would be the#1 mosteffective export to combat repression.

369	('c929c586-231c-4507-ae46-07d772c11936'::uuid,'When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out "not" in them, we lovingly stop, negate them (by removing the "not"), and see if they would weaken.
370	
371	Does it hurt the author if we say, "the mixdoeshave side effects severe enough to make many people avoid it"?
372	
373	Yes, because again the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
374	
375	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause it has severe side effects.','When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out "not" in them, we lovingly stop, negate them (by removing the "not"), and see if they would weaken.
376	
377	Does it hurt the author if we say, "the mix does have side effects severe enough to make many people avoid it"?
378	
379	Yes, because again the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
380	
381	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because it has severe side effects.'),
382	('270cbba5-6dda-4f63-9edc-e6604c83b1e4'::uuid,'Does it hurt the author if we negate this answer and say, "the mix isnotwidely enough know that the vast majority of people would be aware of it" (i.e. a lot of people are not aware of it).
383	
384	Yes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
385	
386	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause they aren''t aware of it (and so have never even tried it in the first place).','Does it hurt the author if we negate this answer and say, "the mix is not widely enough known that the vast majority of people would be aware of it" (i.e. a lot of people are not aware of it).
387	
388	Yes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
389	
390	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because they aren''t aware of it (and so have never even tried it in the first place).'),
391	('cd36c22e-36ba-4161-89c1-b813f9e8a2d1'::uuid,'When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out "no / not" in them, we stop, negate them (by removing the "no / not"), and see if they would weaken.
392	
393	Does it hurt the author if we say, "thereareeffective cold remedies that many people prefer to this mix"?
394	
395	Yes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
396	
397	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause it works but people just prefer a different cold remedy.','When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out "no / not" in them, we stop, negate them (by removing the "no / not"), and see if they would weaken.
398	
399	Does it hurt the author if we say, "there are effective cold remedies that many people prefer to this mix"?
400	
401	Yes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.
402	
403	She has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because it works but people just prefer a different cold remedy.'),
404	('c946f769-2348-4a31-b1c1-04666caa6cdc'::uuid,'We only talked about what was happening inside business schools. We know that they are not very responsive to the needs of business schools, but that doesn''t mean that other people are more responsive. It''s possible that no one is responsive.
405	
406	If I say "children raised in Wyoming are afraid of the dark", does that imply that, "children outside of Wyoming are less afraid of the dark than are children in Wyoming"?
407	
408	Heavens, no! It''s possible that all children are afraid of the dark.
409	
410	So why did I single out Wyoming, then? No reason, I just felt like it. That''s my prerogative as a speaker of truths. I''m allowed to do that. I''m allowed to say "people who were born in Delaware are Americans." That doesn''t mean I''m also saying "people who weren''t born in Delaware aren''t Americans". Necessary Assumption is famous for these Flip a Fact answers.','We only talked about what was happening inside business schools. We know that they are not very responsive to the needs of business, but that doesn''t mean that other people are more responsive. It''s possible that no one is responsive.
411	
412	If I say "children raised in Wyoming are afraid of the dark", does that imply that, "children outside of Wyoming are less afraid of the dark than are children in Wyoming"?
413	
414	Heavens, no! It''s possible that all children are afraid of the dark.
415	
416	So why did I single out Wyoming, then? No reason, I just felt like it. That''s my prerogative as a speaker of truths. I''m allowed to do that. I''m allowed to say "people who were born in Delaware are Americans." That doesn''t mean I''m also saying "people who weren''t born in Delaware aren''t Americans". Necessary Assumption is famous for these Flip a Fact answers.'),
417	('08f177bf-d714-4e3b-9319-5a2413be9596'::uuid,'We are given a conditional
418	
419	advertises products → company''s sales effectively increase
420	
421	To match the original argument, we would want to hear a 2nd premise that says, "recently, a certain company''s sales have increased", and then a Conclusion that says "Thus, products were advertised effectively".
422	
423	Instead, this argument is actually reasoning validly through the contrapositive.
424	
425	It''s saying that since the Outcome of the rule isn''t the case (the sales have not increased) that the Trigger must not be the case (the companydidn''tadvertise effectively).','We are given a conditional
426	
427	advertises products effectively → company''s sales increase
428	
429	To match the original argument, we would want to hear a 2nd premise that says, "recently, a certain company''s sales have increased", and then a Conclusion that says "Thus, products were advertised effectively".
430	
431	Instead, this argument is actually reasoning validly through the contrapositive.
432	
433	It''s saying that since the Outcome of the rule isn''t the case (the sales have not increased) that the Trigger must not be the case (the company didn''t advertise effectively).'),
434	('368ef6df-ce25-4a0e-a76a-72591f57c54f'::uuid,'We can make a similar objection like: "Sure, each individual entry is unlikely to be X, but for sureoneof the entries will be X".
435	
436	We can say, "Sure each individual card is unlikely to be an ace, but for suresomecard drawn will be an ace".
437	
438	(Annoyingly, this answer does not specify that we''re talking about a complete deck of cards and assumes some outside knowledge that there is at least one ace in every deck)','We can make a similar objection like: "Sure, each individual entry is unlikely to be X, but for sure one of the entries will be X".
439	
440	We can say, "Sure each individual card is unlikely to be an ace, but for sure some card drawn will be an ace".
441	
442	(Annoyingly, this answer does not specify that we''re talking about a complete deck of cards and assumes some outside knowledge that there is at least one ace in every deck)'),
443	('2c2d9031-018b-449c-b690-2f073ab2ea5d'::uuid,'If one horse has a 99.9% chance of winning, then it''s absolutely reasonable to believe that no one other than that horsewillwin. It''s another thing to say that no one other than that horsecanwin.
444	
445	But that''s a different flaw. That''s just pointing out a technicality of language. We''d be saying, "Even though it''s virtually certain that Horse X will win, it''s not 100% certain, so you can''t say that other horsescan''twin." The fact that something is EXTREMELY unlikely doesn''t make it impossible. This answer is saying that "only one thing can be the winner" whereas the original conclusion was saying "zero things can be the winner". And in this argument, one horse has a huge advantage over all the other horses in the race. In the original, no lottery ticket had any advantage over all the other tickets in the lottery. This answer is trying to make people enticed for shallow, superficial reasons. It regurgitates the number 1000.','If one horse has a 99.9% chance of winning, then it''s absolutely reasonable to believe that no one other than that horse will win. It''s another thing to say that no one other than that horse can win.
446	
447	But that''s a different flaw. That''s just pointing out a technicality of language. We''d be saying, "Even though it''s virtually certain that Horse X will win, it''s not 100% certain, so you can''t say that other horses can''t win." The fact that something is EXTREMELY unlikely doesn''t make it impossible. This answer is saying that "only one thing can be the winner" whereas the original conclusion was saying "zero things can be the winner". And in this argument, one horse has a huge advantage over all the other horses in the race. In the original, no lottery ticket had any advantage over all the other tickets in the lottery. This answer is trying to make people enticed for shallow, superficial reasons. It regurgitates the number 1000.')
448	) v(id,old,new) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.old returning t.id;

As we mentioned before, the correct answer to Agree is usually some background fact / assumption that both people implicitly recognize.\n\nWe can tell that Liang thinks that the effects of dramatic depictions of violence are at least partially understood, because Liang acts as though it''s a known fact that \"watching depictions of violence as an appropriate way to resolve problems has the effect of increasing levels of aggression in viewers\".\n\nAnd we know Sarah would agree because she acts as though it''s a known fact that \"watching depictions of violent characters has the effect of letting the audience experience aggressive emotions and purge them.\"\n\nSince they both cited \"causal facts\" about the effects of violence in movies, they must both think that we at least partially understand the effects of violence in movies.','This answer is abstract, but it fits the pattern. As we mentioned before, the correct answer to Agree is usually some background fact / assumption that both people implicitly recognize.\n\nWe can tell that Liang thinks that the effects of dramatic depictions of violence are at least partially understood, because Liang acts as though it''s a known fact that \"watching depictions of violence as an appropriate way to resolve problems has the effect of increasing levels of aggression in viewers\".\n\nAnd we know Sarah would agree because she acts as though it''s a known fact that \"watching depictions of violent characters has the effect of letting the audience experience aggressive emotions and purge them.\"\n\nSince they both cited \"causal facts\" about the effects of violence in movies, they must both think that we at least partially understand the effects of violence in movies.'),\n('be1cd37b-da70-4d59-a63a-fcd2dc6b2447'::uuid,'This answer is terribly written, but it wins by the standard of best available. It''s certainly similar in sentiment to what we were looking for, \"the Nobel rules unfairly exclude some deserving people from getting a Nobel\".\n\nBut it feels like a backwards version of what we want. This answer sounds like \"having a Nobel doesn''t mean (doesn''t accurately indicate) that you deserved one for your contribution\".\n\nHowever, that''s not what they were saying. They were saying, \"not getting a Nobel doesn''t mean you didn''t deserve one for your contribution.\"\n\nApparently LSAT was hoping we would hear this answer in a fuzzier way, like, \"Whether you have or lack a Nobel is not an accurate indicator of your contributions to science.\"\n\nBut it''s pretty obnoxiously sloppy on their part. Again, keep that mantra of \"best available\" in your head. Occasionally, you may even have to think, \"Sigh. This question is technically broken, but what do I think the test writers wanted the credited response to be?\"','This answer is abstract, but it matches what we were looking for, \"the Nobel rules unfairly exclude some deserving people from getting a Nobel\".\n\nBoth speakers point to people whose work deserves a prize but who can''t get one under the rules: the fourth or fifth scientist behind an important result, or a scientist who died before the work was appreciated.\n\nIf deserving contributors can go without a Nobel, then who has a Nobel and who doesn''t is not an accurate picture of who contributed what. That''s what this answer says. It doesn''t require that prizes ever go to undeserving people. Missing deserving people is enough to make Nobel Prizes inaccurate indicators.'),\n('15c9d331-6065-45fc-a5f8-d813d9fd3614'::uuid,'The dispute is over whether Stalin''s cruel misdeeds and morbid suspiciousness in his later years provide evidence of him going insane.','Both sides seem to accept that Stalin''s misdeeds reflect morbid suspiciousness. The writers use that very term for Stalin''s vindictiveness, and the critic doesn''t deny it. The critic says morbid suspiciousness is simply a characteristic every tyrant needs to stay in power.\n\nThe dispute is over what that suspiciousness shows: the writers treat it as a sign that Stalin''s sanity was slipping in the later years, while the critic treats it as a normal feature of tyranny that was there all along.'),\n('e9f5e3a4-3d0f-442c-9c84-6cfc814e6bdf'::uuid,'What we know of Justine is that she believes this connection to be true:\nif you settle out of court, the corporate leaders expected to lose\nCorporate leaders didn''t → wouldn''t have settled\nexpect to lose out of court\nSo we know Justine would agree with this answer choice.\nWould Simon disagree? Disagreeing with a conditional means that you''re saying \"it''s possible the left side is true but the right side isn''t\".\nWas Simon saying that, \"It''s possible that the corporate leaders did expect to win, but still would have chosen to settle\"?\nYes! He''s saying there are circumstances where even if you expect to win, you see that you''ll be paying more in legal fees than you''d get out of the settlement, so you settle just to because it''s a financially preferable outcome.','What we know of Justine is that she believes this connection to be true:\nif you settle out of court, the corporate leaders expected to lose\nIts contrapositive:\nCorporate leaders didn''t expect to lose → wouldn''t have settled out of court\nSo we know Justine would agree with this answer choice.\nWould Simon disagree? Disagreeing with a conditional means that you''re saying \"it''s possible the left side is true but the right side isn''t\".\nWas Simon saying that, \"It''s possible that the corporate leaders did expect to win, but still would have chosen to settle\"?\nYes! He''s saying there are circumstances where even if you expect to win, you see that you''ll be paying more in legal fees than the settlement would cost, so you settle just because it''s a financially preferable outcome.'),\n('0979622a-a6de-46e5-9092-bc86f002e55d'::uuid,'Justine believes this connection to be true:\nif you settle out of court, the corporate leaders expected to lose\nThis answer, by contrapositive, says\nif you settle out of court, then you thought that the legal fees would be at least as costly as the settlement.\nWe can''t say she agrees with the answer, because obviously those two outcomes are saying completely different things. The triggers match, but the outcomes are far from synonyms.\nTo disagree with the answer is to say, \"Sometimes people settle out of court, but they think the legal fees will be less costly than the settlement.\"\nCan we support that Justine believes this Disagree Position?\nNo, because she never even commented on people''s perceptions of legal feels in relation to settlement size.\nWe also don''t really know Simon''s position, although he seems close to agreeing with this. The triggers match, but the outcomes are far from synonyms.\nTo disagree with the answer is to say, \"Sometimes people settle out of court, but they think the legal fees will be less costly than the settlement.\"\nCan we support that Justine believes this Disagree Position?\nNo, because she never even commented on people''s perceptions of legal fees in relation to settlement size.\nWe also don''t really know Simon''s position, although he seems close to agreeing with this. It doesn''t seem like either person supported any universal generalization about things that aren''t genuinely autonomous disciplines.\n\nIf we contraposed this answer to match the language of the paragraphs a little better, we''d get something like this:\n\nUnique domain of inquiry genuinely or → autonomous Unique methodology discipline\n\nNeither person would sign off on a conditional like that. If Mario agreed to that rule, he would think that any field of study with a unique methodology (whether or not it had a unique domain of inquiry) was a genuinely autonomous discipline.\n\nBut he definitely doesn''t believe that. And Lucy would be agreeing to a rule that says any field of study with a domain of its own is automatically a genuinely autonomous discipline, which she clearly does not believe.\n\nConditional Logic note:The word \"any\" is sufficient indicator, so this is saying~unique meth not genuinely auto disc → and~unique domain\n\nThat''s why when we contraposed this rule we ended up with an Or on the left side.','We might correctly find this unappealing at a glance, because it''s a universal about all non-genuinely autonomous disciplines. It doesn''t seem like either person supported any universal generalization about things that aren''t genuinely autonomous disciplines.\n\nIf we contraposed this answer to match the language of the paragraphs a little better, we''d get something like this:\n\nUnique domain of inquiry or Unique methodology → genuinely autonomous discipline\n\nNeither person would sign off on a conditional like that. And Lucy would be agreeing to a rule that says any field of study with a domain of its own is automatically a genuinely autonomous discipline, which she clearly does not believe.\n\nConditional Logic note: The word \"any\" is sufficient indicator, so this is saying\nnot genuinely auto disc → ~unique meth and ~unique domain\n\nThat''s why when we contraposed this rule we ended up with an Or on the left side.'),\n('46c4bae6-3e16-43de-ba83-8201828b046c'::uuid,'We know that G agrees because she explicitly says \"They (the striking workers) are underpaid.\"\n\nWe can support that N disagrees because he says, \"Well, if pay is your concern, I''m out. After all... ) the average salary of the striking workers is over $29k.\"'),\n('0fc175c5-e6b6-4c5a-8c72-ad3870ceda11'::uuid,'Does the author need this specific math relationship to be true? Would it matter if some hours devoted to teaching preventative medicine reduce by 9% and other hours reduce by 11%?\n\nThe 10% in this answer choice matches the idea that the time med school spend on preventative is 10% of what they spend on curative.\n\nThe author is just saying that they spend 10% as much time on curative, even though curative cuts down medical costs \"greatly\".\n\nThose two claims don''t commit the author to believing some weirdly specific idea that each hour of preventative teaching reduces costs by at least 10%.\n\nEven if every hour of preventative teaching reduced costs by only 5%, the author''s conclusion could still be true --- spending more time on preventative medicine could still help achieve the goal of making medicine more cost-effective.','Does the author need this specific math relationship to be true? Would it matter if some hours devoted to teaching preventative medicine reduce by 9% and other hours reduce by 11%?\n\nThe 10% in this answer choice matches the idea that the time med school spend on preventative is 10% of what they spend on curative.\n\nThe author is just saying that they spend 10% as much time on preventative, even though preventative cuts down medical costs \"greatly\".\n\nThose two claims don''t commit the author to believing some weirdly specific idea that each hour of preventative teaching reduces costs by at least 10%.\n\nEven if every hour of preventative teaching reduced costs by only 5%, the author''s conclusion could still be true --- spending more time on preventative medicine could still help achieve the goal of making medicine more cost-effective.'),\n('4a86f571-3d74-410f-ae3c-a2122f31efe1'::uuid,'In 99% of cases, the word “most” is wrong on Necessary Assumption. But in this case, the idea of “a majority / most people” is logically important because the conclusion is about “most people who commit embezzlement / bribery”.\nIn fact, we could even have thought to ourselves, “There’s a New Term in the conclusion! It’s talking about most people who commit embezzlement / bribery, but the evidence never defines or discusses that group. Therefore, this new term should be in my correct answer.”\nIf we negate this, it’s saying, “Most people who commit these two crimes do not do so repeatedly.” Why would that weaken?\nBecause … the trigger that starts this whole causal chain is motion is the idea in the first sentence that if you repeatedly commit these crimes and don’t get caught, you’ll end up being more confident, which will lead to commit these crimes again, which will keep increasing the odds that you eventually get caught.\nBut if most people who commit these two crimes only do so once, then the author doesn’t get that causal chain he’s envisioning, in which the repeat offender keeps increasing the odds of being caught. If most people who commit these crimes do so only once, then they can just commit the crime, not get caught, and be done. Thus, they are not likely to eventually get caught.','In 99% of cases, the word “most” is wrong on Necessary Assumption. But in this case, the idea of “a majority / most people” is logically important because the conclusion is about “most people who commit embezzlement / bribery”.\nIn fact, we could even have thought to ourselves, “There’s a New Term in the conclusion! It’s talking about most people who commit embezzlement / bribery, but the evidence never defines or discusses that group. Therefore, this new term should be in my correct answer.”\nIf we negate this, it’s saying, “Most people who commit these two crimes do not do so repeatedly.” Why would that weaken?\nBecause … the trigger that starts this whole causal chain in motion is the idea in the first sentence that if you repeatedly commit these crimes and don’t get caught, you’ll end up being more confident, which will lead to commit these crimes again, which will keep increasing the odds that you eventually get caught.\nBut if most people who commit these two crimes only do so once, then the author doesn’t get that causal chain he’s envisioning, in which the repeat offender keeps increasing the odds of being caught. If most people who commit these crimes do so only once, then they can just commit the crime, not get caught, and be done. Thus, they are not likely to eventually get caught.'),\n('f10c6c6c-1d48-4f04-a777-389143732997'::uuid,'\"The only\" is strong, and thus a red flag, but also conditional, and thus something we should examine by looking at it conditionally (as well as its contrapositive).\n\n\"The only\" is a sufficient, Left-side indicator word.\n(only and only if are necessary, Right-side indicator words)\n\nSo the idea following \"the only\", a means of making itself unpalatable to predators, would go on the Left of the arrow.\n\n```\na way to make itself      feeding on\nhighly unpalatable    →   the Natal\nto predators              grass cycad\n```\n\nWhen correct answers are conditional, they''re usually written in contrapositive form (to be sneaky, to disguise their potential appeal). When we contrapose this one, we get:\n\n```\ncan''t feed on the        no means to make\nNatal grass cycad    →   itself highly yucky\n                         to predators\n```\n\nDid the author''s argument make that move?\n\nSure. Not explicitly, but that''s definitely the intended meaning of how the author goes from thinking, \"Oh, no, the plant that gives them their anti-predator toxin is endangered. Thus they are endangered.\"\n\nHe must be thinking that without this plant, the moth loses this defense mechanism, and thus the predators will be able to gobble them up to the point where their species will be in danger of surviving.\n\nThis answer choice is just calling out that first thought, that \"without this plant, the moth will lose the defense mechanism of making itself taste yucky.\"\n\nIf we negate this answer and say, \"the cycad isn''t the only way the moth can make itself unpalatable. There is at least one other way to make itself unpalatable\", then that would badly weaken the argument, since it would make it seem like the moths aren''t so vulnerable to predators all of a sudden.','\"The only\" is strong, and thus a red flag, but also conditional, and thus something we should examine by looking at it conditionally (as well as its contrapositive).\n\n\"The only\" is a sufficient, Left-side indicator word.\n(only and only if are necessary, Right-side indicator words)\n\nSo the idea following \"the only\", a means of making itself unpalatable to predators, would go on the Left of the arrow.\n\n```\na way to make itself      feeding on\nhighly unpalatable    →   the Natal\nto predators              grass cycad\n```\n\nWhen correct answers are conditional, they''re usually written in contrapositive form (to be sneaky, to disguise their potential appeal). When we contrapose this one, we get:\n\n```\ncan''t feed on the        no means to make\nNatal grass cycad    →   itself highly yucky\n                         to predators\n```\n\nDid the author''s argument make that move?\n\nSure. Not explicitly, but that''s definitely the intended meaning of how the author goes from thinking, \"Oh, no, the plant that gives them their anti-predator toxin is endangered. Thus they are endangered.\"\n\nHe must be thinking that without this plant, the moth loses this defense mechanism, and thus the predators will be able to gobble them up to the point where their species will be in danger of not surviving.\n\nThis answer choice is just calling out that first thought, that \"without this plant, the moth will lose the defense mechanism of making itself taste yucky.\"\n\nIf we negate this answer and say, \"the cycad isn''t the only way the moth can make itself unpalatable. There is at least one other way to make itself unpalatable\", then that would badly weaken the argument, since it would make it seem like the moths aren''t so vulnerable to predators all of a sudden.'),\n('42674041-f230-4041-bd26-dff1ada92f6a'::uuid,'This has the lovable ruling-out language \"cannot\", no let''s negate it and see if it weakens:\n\nPredators can determine from appearance alone whether a moth''s body is laced with the toxin.\n\nNo, that would actually strengthen. When they cycad runs out, and the moth is now flying around toxin-free, it wouldn''t have the temporary protection of its predators assuming that it still tastes nasty like they''ve always known it to.\n\nInstead, this negation is saying they would visually see that the moth wasn''t wearing its \"yuck suit\" anymore, so it would be open season on hunting the moths.','This has the lovable ruling-out language \"cannot\", so let''s negate it and see if it weakens:\n\nPredators can determine from appearance alone whether a moth''s body is laced with the toxin.\n\nNo, that would actually strengthen. When they cycad runs out, and the moth is now flying around toxin-free, it wouldn''t have the temporary protection of its predators assuming that it still tastes nasty like they''ve always known it to.\n\nInstead, this negation is saying they would visually see that the moth wasn''t wearing its \"yuck suit\" anymore, so it would be open season on hunting the moths.'),\n('feb095d8-cbcb-48de-8086-e81a4521be2d'::uuid,'If we ever see the Conclusion as part of the trigger, then the answer is automatically wrong.\n\nFor example, if an answer said,\n(A) If the public is unafraid of scientists, then ...\n\nWe''d immediately eliminate it. The conclusion is supposed to be on the right side (the outcome) of a conditional reasoning move.\n\nWe want to see\npremise → conclusion\n\nHowever, when you contrapose that reasoning move (as so many correct answers do), you get:\nnot conclusion → not premise\n\nSo seeing the negation of the conclusion in the trigger is a great sign. That''s what we''re seeing here. The answer says,\n\n```\nif public is afraid          over several decades\nof scientists          →     a scientist would meet\n                             someone afraid of sci''s\n```\n\nAnd the contrapositive of this looks just like the Premise → Conclusion move of the argument!\n\n```\nIf over several decades       public is not\na scientist has not met   →   afraid of\nsomeone afraid of sci''s       scientists\n```','If we see the Conclusion itself as the trigger, the answer is almost certainly wrong.\n\nFor example, if an answer said,\nSample answer: If the public is unafraid of scientists, then ...\n\nWe''d immediately eliminate it. The conclusion is supposed to be on the right side (the outcome) of a conditional reasoning move.\n\nWe want to see\npremise → conclusion\n\nHowever, when you contrapose that reasoning move (as so many correct answers do), you get:\nnot conclusion → not premise\n\nSo seeing the negation of the conclusion in the trigger is a great sign. That''s what we''re seeing here. The answer says,\n\n```\nif public is afraid          over several decades\nof scientists          →     a scientist would meet\n                             someone afraid of sci''s\n```\n\nAnd the contrapositive of this looks just like the Premise → Conclusion move of the argument!\n\n```\nIf over several decades       public is not\na scientist has not met   →   afraid of\nsomeone afraid of sci''s       scientists\n```'),\n('a0a73a22-677b-4e88-91fe-f5baeb285b9a'::uuid,'If we negate this \"not / no\" and get, \"babies do pay more attention to phrases with simple structures\", does that weaken the argument?\n\nNot in any clear way. This is talking about paying more attention not being helped extra when it comes to learning the language.\n\nAnd the answer is just talking about any simply-structured phrase, not repeating simple phrases. Our author might agree that it''s better to speak to babies with simple structures because they pay more attention, but still say that repeating these simple phrases has no learning value.','If we negate this \"not / no\" and get, \"babies do pay more attention to phrases with simple structures\", does that weaken the argument?\n\nNot in any clear way. Paying more attention is not the same as being helped extra when it comes to learning the language, and the argument is only about being helped extra.\n\nAnd the answer is just talking about any simply-structured phrase, not repeating simple phrases. Our author might agree that it''s better to speak to babies with simple structures because they pay more attention, but still say that repeating these simple phrases has no learning value.'),\n('c405bcc2-0c2a-40f1-a080-33924a54fbd7'::uuid,'This has the lovable quality of ruling-out with a \"not / no\", which is so common among correct answers on Necessary Assumption.\n\nWhen we see these ruled-out ideas, we should use the Negation test. If we remove the \"not / no\", does this answer turn into an objection?\n\nThe telecom equip imported into Swould beavailable solely to top government officials.\n\nHeck yeah that''s an objection. The manufacturer is suggesting that by exporting phones and faxes, we can get to where telecom equipment is widely available to the population of S, at which point repression is impossible.\n\nBut when we negate this answer, it''s saying, \"Yo, manufacturers -- what if the leaders of S don''t allow these exports to be disseminated widely among the population. If the leaders keep all the phones and faxes exclusively to themselves, then we''ve basically just given them a gift when we''re supposed to be isolating them and depriving them of things.\"\n\nAnother way to think about this answer is that the manufacturers were clearly thinking that by exporting phones and faxes, the result would be that telecom equipment is widely available to the people of S, and in order for that to be true, it would have to be the case that the government doesn''t keep all that imported telecom equipment exclusively for itself.','This has the lovable quality of ruling-out with a \"not / no\", which is so common among correct answers on Necessary Assumption.\n\nWhen we see these ruled-out ideas, we should use the Negation test. If we remove the \"not / no\", does this answer turn into an objection?\n\nThe telecom equip imported into S would be available solely to top government officials.\n\nHeck yeah that''s an objection. The manufacturer is suggesting that by exporting phones and faxes, we can get to where telecom equipment is widely available to the population of S, at which point repression is impossible.\n\nBut when we negate this answer, it''s saying, \"Yo, manufacturers -- what if the leaders of S don''t allow these exports to be disseminated widely among the population. If the leaders keep all the phones and faxes exclusively to themselves, then we''ve basically just given them a gift when we''re supposed to be isolating them and depriving them of things.\"\n\nAnother way to think about this answer is that the manufacturers were clearly thinking that by exporting phones and faxes, the result would be that telecom equipment is widely available to the people of S, and in order for that to be true, it would have to be the case that the government doesn''t keep all that imported telecom equipment exclusively for itself.'),\n('dcd39393-f9bd-4f1c-9f28-98921726bc55'::uuid,'The word/concept of \"most\" is wrong on Necessary Assumption, 98% of the time we see it, since the difference between most and not-most is a usually-meaningless move from at least 51% to at most 49%.\n\nFurthermore, if we negate this answer, it would strengthen, which is not how a correct answer would behave. The negation says that a majority of legislatorsdofavor exempting telecom equipment, so a majority of legislators agree with the conclusion.\n\nThe negation of the correct answer should weaken, not strengthen.','The word/concept of \"most\" is wrong on Necessary Assumption, 98% of the time we see it, since the difference between most and not-most is a usually-meaningless move from at least 51% to at most 49%.\n\nFurthermore, if we negate this answer, it would strengthen, which is not how a correct answer would behave. The negation says that a majority of legislators do favor exempting telecom equipment, so a majority of legislators agree with the conclusion.\n\nThe negation of the correct answer should weaken, not strengthen.'),\n('e724ea31-5bed-4781-928c-3adf29d8ae3d'::uuid,'We don''t have any language to support this superlative claim that telecom equipment would be the#1 mosteffective export to combat repression.\n\nThe manufacturers haven''t said anything like, \"onlyour products should be exempted\". see if they would weaken.\n\nDoes it hurt the author if we say, \"the mixdoeshave side effects severe enough to make many people avoid it\"?\n\nYes, because again the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause it has severe side effects.','When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out \"not\" in them, we lovingly stop, negate them (by removing the \"not\"), and see if they would weaken.\n\nDoes it hurt the author if we say, \"the mix does have side effects severe enough to make many people avoid it\"?\n\nYes, because again the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because it has severe side effects.'),\n('270cbba5-6dda-4f63-9edc-e6604c83b1e4'::uuid,'Does it hurt the author if we negate this answer and say, \"the mix isnotwidely enough know that the vast majority of people would be aware that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause they aren''t aware of it (and so have never even tried it in the first place).','Does it hurt the author if we negate this answer and say, \"the mix is not widely enough known that the vast majority of people would be aware of it\" (i.e. a lot of people are not aware of it).\n\nYes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because they aren''t aware of it (and so have never even tried it in the first place).'),\n('cd36c22e-36ba-4161-89c1-b813f9e8a2d1'::uuid,'When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out \"no / not\" in them, we stop, negate them (by removing the \"no / not\"), and see if they would weaken.\n\nDoes it hurt the author if we say, \"thereareeffective cold remedies that many people prefer to this mix\"?\n\nYes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work,notbecause it works but people just prefer a different cold remedy.','When Necessary Assumption answer choices have a ruling out \"no / not\" in them, we stop, negate them (by removing the \"no / not\"), and see if they would weaken.\n\nDoes it hurt the author if we say, \"there are effective cold remedies that many people prefer to this mix\"?\n\nYes, because the author is blaming the mix''s lack of use on its ineffectiveness, but if we offer an alternate explanation for its lack of use, that weakens her argument.\n\nShe has to assume people are avoiding it because it doesn''t work, not because it works but people just prefer a different cold remedy.'),\n('c946f769-2348-4a31-b1c1-04666caa6cdc'::uuid,'We only talked about what was happening inside business schools. We know that they are not very responsive to the needs of business schools, but that doesn''t mean that other people are more responsive. It''s possible that no one is responsive.\n\nIf I say \"children raised in Wyoming are afraid of the dark\", does that imply that, \"children outside of Wyoming are less afraid of the dark than are children in Wyoming\"?\n\nHeavens, no! It''s possible that all children are afraid of the dark.\n\nSo why did I single out Wyoming, then? No reason, I just felt like it. That''s my prerogative as a speaker of truths. I''m allowed to do that. I''m allowed to say \"people who were born in Delaware are Americans.\" That doesn''t mean I''m also saying \"people who weren''t born in Delaware aren''t Americans\". Necessary Assumption is famous for these Flip a Fact answers.','We only talked about what was happening inside business schools. We know that they are not very responsive to the needs of business, but that doesn''t mean that other people are more responsive. It''s possible that no one is responsive.\n\nIf I say \"children raised in Wyoming are afraid of the dark\", does that imply that, \"children outside of Wyoming are less afraid of the dark than are children in Wyoming\"?\n\nHeavens, no! It''s possible that all children are afraid of the dark.\n\nSo why did I single out Wyoming, then? No reason, I just felt like it. That''s my prerogative as a speaker of truths. I''m allowed to do that. I''m allowed to say \"people who were born in Delaware are Americans.\" That doesn''t mean I''m also saying \"people who weren''t born in Delaware aren''t Americans\". Necessary Assumption is famous for these Flip a Fact answers.'),\n('08f177bf-d714-4e3b-9319-5a2413be9596'::uuid,'We are given a conditional\n\nadvertises products → company''s sales effectively increase\n\nTo match the original argument, we would want to hear a 2nd premise that says, \"recently, a certain company''s sales have increased\", and then a Conclusion that says \"Thus, products 146	('a3328c66-d6cc-49f4-b43d-532c2901af44'::uuid,'**Only**  = right side idea, so just like (A), we would contrapose so that the outcome of the conditional looks like the conclusion.

394	('047382df-8465-4cad-b714-c1a352e80b22'::uuid,'Well this was easier than one might expect. This is the only answer that has the missing trigger language from the principle.
395	
396	It''s not a perfect answer, but it''s the best available when none of the other ones did anything related to our task. This rule says:
397	
398	if person/business provides a service that can be expected to be used to infringe on a copyright → then they should be considered to knowingly aid any infringer using it
399	
400	Can we say, when it comes to a "self-service photo-printing kiosk", that a person or business can expect that service to be used to infringe on a copyright?
401	
402	I mean ... the information we were given didn''t establish that. But, yes, it''s a fairly reasonable thing in the real-world to think, "If I give people the ability to make photo-quality prints, are some people going to use it to infringe on copyrights? Probably."
403	
404	If this rule applies to GDS''s self-serve photo kiosk, then it establishes that they knowingly aided the customer who infringed on a copyright, which means we can apply the Principle and derive that they are also guilty of copyright infringement.
405	
406	It''s pretty shocking that LSAC would ask us to pick an answer like this, where the trigger is only applicable if we make a somewhat tenuous real-world judgment on the matter. But since the other four are structurally useless to us, it''s hard to resist the idea that this is the best available answer.','Well this was easier than one might expect. This is the only answer that has the missing trigger language from the principle.
407

409

411	
412	Can we say, when it comes to a "self-service photo-printing kiosk", that a person or business can expect that service to be used to infringe on a copyright?
413	
414	I mean ... the information we were given didn''t establish that. But, yes, it''s a fairly reasonable thing in the real-world to think, "If I give people the ability to make photo-quality prints, are some people going to use it to infringe on copyrights? Probably."
415	
416	If this rule applies to GDS''s self-serve photo kiosk, then it establishes that they knowingly aided the customer who infringed on a copyright, which means we can apply the Principle and derive that they are also guilty of copyright infringement.

419	('3690ab9d-c868-4539-8763-5a544b90271c'::uuid,'This is a rule about whether or not an action should be legally permissible. 422	('08f1ad3f-20da-4ce2-87b6-7fa91e6124db'::uuid,'This is very attractive, since it matches our trigger, "disproportionately burden low incomes".

425	Even if we knew road maintenance costs were going up when we reintroduce rock salt, do we know if it would change local sales taxes?
426	We don''t. 430	We don''t. Road maintenance is primarily funded by local sales taxes, but we can''t assume that reintroducing rock salt will boost the road maintenance budget so much that local sales taxes also go up.')

If you interpret the premise to be saying \"it''s unreasonable to believe that 1000 consecutive coin flips willeverturn up heads\", then it''s fine to conclude \"it''s reasonable to think 1000 flips willneverall be heads\".\n\nIt''s not impossible, because the probability of it happening (1/2 to the 1000th power) is nonzero. Maybe the results of the planned experiment will not match Dr. Grippen''s or Professor Heissmann''s predictions.'),\n('a3328c66-d6cc-49f4-b43d-532c2901af44'::uuid,'**Only**  = right side idea, so just like (A), we would contrapose so that the outcome of the conditional looks like the conclusion.\n\n*if those accepting X              knowingly*\n*as true suffer a hardship  →  making false*\n*greater than the gain              claim X is*\n*they were anticipating          not unethical*\n\nThe outcome matches our conclusion. Is the trigger applicable to our situation?\n\nWere we told that people who bought this book while believing it really would show them how to be exceptionally successful will suffer a hardship greater than the gain they were anticipating?\n\nWe were not. And it seems unlikely that the trigger would apply to these book buyers. After all, the gain they were anticipating (if they believed the hype) is **exceptional success**.\n\nThat''s a huge gain! Is the hardship they suffer from finding out the book is not life-changing *greater* than that? Is it a huge, life-changing hardship?\n\nNo, it''s just a waste of $20.','**Only**  = right side idea, so just like (A), we would contrapose so that the outcome of the conditional looks like the conclusion.\n\n*if those accepting X as true do not suffer a hardship greater than the gain they were anticipating → knowingly making false claim X is not unethical*\n\nThe outcome matches our conclusion. Is the trigger applicable to our situation?\n\nWere we told anything about the hardship suffered by people who believed this book would make them exceptionally successful, or how that hardship compares with the gain they were hoping for?\n\nWe were not. The argument''s only reason is that everyone knows no book can deliver exceptional success. Hardships and gains never come up, so this principle doesn''t connect to the reasoning the author actually gives. (A) does, because \"everyone knows the claim is false\" is exactly a case where it isn''t reasonable to accept the claim as true.'),\n('c3fa958b-a4bc-43b4-b686-aede949412f6'::uuid,'Neither of the situations clearly establish the trigger for this rule, so this rule isn''t useful for applying to either George or Carla''s situation:\n\nAll alternative strategies physical discipline for correcting bad → is justified behavior have failed\n\nWe don''t know whether George or Carla exhausted all alternative strategies before trying physical discipline, so we can''t apply this rule to either of their situations.','Neither of the situations lets us use this rule to reach the judgments:\n\nphysical discipline is justified → all alternative strategies for correcting bad behavior have failed\n\n\"Only when\" introduces the necessary condition, so this rule can only prove that physical discipline was NOT justified, by showing that some alternative strategy had not yet been tried and failed. It can never prove that discipline was justified, so it can''t support the judgment about Carla.\n\nIt doesn''t help with George either. George made several attempts to distract the parrot before hitting it, and we''re never told of an alternative George skipped, so we can''t use this rule to show that George''s hitting was unjustified.'),\n('1e9c0c42-18bf-4616-b58f-d41b7572703c'::uuid,'This gets at the salient difference the author cared about between George''s situation and Carla''s, whether or not the action the pet was committing was the type of action that could hurt the pet.\n\nThis answer would basically prove the judgment about George''s situation, because it gives us this rule:\n\nBehavior X would not result → Should not use in serious harm to the animal phys discipline\n\nChewing on furniture would not result in serious harm to the parrot, so George should not have used physical discipline.\n\nThis rule doesn''t prove the judgment about Carla because it''s only a rule that canprove\"shouldnotuse discipline\". I can''t really remember another example of that.\n\nThe word \"for\" in the first sentence (of FABS fame ... for, after all, because, since) shows that the first clause is a conclusion being supported by what comes after \"for\".\n\nThe author is indicating that whether or not a novel has much social significance is related to whether or not readers can enter the internal world of the novelist''s mind.\n\nThe author''s overall argument is trying to say that, when it comes to contemporary novels ...\n\nmoral transgressions are not events whose purpose is to be seen as moral injustices,\nand thus\nyou don''t experience the novel''s world from the moral perspective of the novel''s characters\nand thus\nyou can''t cant enter the internal world of the novelist''s mind,\nand thus\nthere is little of social significance.\n\nThis answer provides that last link.','This is one of those original two assumptions we pulled out of the first sentence.
