I can''t really remember another example of that.\n\nThe word \"for\" in the first sentence (of FABS fame ... for, after all, because, since) shows that the first clause is a conclusion being supported by what comes after \"for\".\n\nThe author is indicating that whether or not a novel has much social significance is related to whether or not readers can enter the internal world of the novelist''s mind.\n\nThe author''s overall argument is trying to say that, when it comes to contemporary novels ...\n\nmoral transgressions are not events whose purpose is to be seen as moral injustices,\nand thus\nyou don''t experience the novel''s world from the moral perspective of the novel''s characters\nand thus\nyou can''t enter the internal world of the novelist''s mind,\nand thus\nthere is little of social significance.\n\nThis answer provides that last link.'),\n('a08ed79d-3beb-4110-a020-0f2320d67d8a'::uuid,'This answer doesn''t sound like it provides a positive or a negative. It just asserts a need that a certain population has (it''s also presumably a need that illiterate, oral populations would also have, to make this answer even less impactful).\n\nWe need a rule that takes what we know about the differences between Oral vs. Written traditions and deems the former the morepreferableone.','This answer doesn''t sound like it provides a positive or a negative. It just asserts a need that a certain population has (it''s also presumably a need that illiterate, oral populations would also have, to make this answer even less impactful).\n\nWe need a rule that takes what we know about the differences between Oral vs. Written traditions and deems the former the more preferable one.'),\n('c0fa8f19-ec53-4a6c-95e6-d33a19a11966'::uuid,'With \"greater value\", we finally have answer choice language that could help us argue that one tradition ispreferableto another.\n\nBut in order to use this to judge the Oral vs. Written head-to-head, we''ve have to be thinking, Oral traditions = tradition Written traditions = accumulation of knowledge\n\nBut that doesn''t make any sense.\n\nFirst of all, both oral traditions and written traditions aretraditions. Secondly, \"accumulation of knowledge\" doesn''t even match anything we read about.\n\nTheproliferation ofwritingcertainly doesn''t mean the same thing as the accumulation ofknowledge.','With \"greater value\", we finally have answer choice language that could help us argue that one tradition is preferable to another.\n\nBut in order to use this to judge the Oral vs. Written head-to-head, we''d have to be thinking,\nOral traditions = tradition\nWritten traditions = accumulation of knowledge\n\nBut that doesn''t make any sense.\n\nFirst of all, both oral traditions and written traditions are traditions. Secondly, \"accumulation of knowledge\" doesn''t even match anything we read about.\n\nThe proliferation of writing certainly doesn''t mean the same thing as the accumulation of knowledge.'),\n('3cf8cf0d-cef4-4c6b-8052-6aa4a72308a5'::uuid,'This answer has the clearest connection to our conclusion language,preferred = preferable.\n\nWhen we''re doing Strengthen + Principle, our biggest shortcut is to only consider answers (at least initially) that give us wording that match up with our conclusion.\n\nIn order to use this rule to weigh in on the Oral vs. Written head-to-head, we need to think Oral traditions = economy of expression Written traditions = verbosity\n\nThis is what the 2nd advantage of oral traditions was getting at. Oral traditions trim the fluff -- they eradicate the useless and irrelevant.\n\nWhile reading the paragraph, I interpreted these last two sentences to mean that in literate societies, there issomuch writing that you fill up libraries and the reader doesn''t know what to read / what to believe / whom to trust. Meanwhile, in oral societies, they can only remember their top 100 stories / tales / instructional metaphors... so it''s sort of a survival of the fittest ideas.\n\nBut what this answer choice makes me think LSAC was really intending with the last two sentences is this: - Writing has no limits, so I can keep adding clauses and semicolons and modifiers and such until I have such a long, dense, poorly written sentence that the writer and reader are confused on the ultimate meaning of that sentence.(verbosity)\n\n- Oral traditions would make it really hard to remember a super long, complicated sentence. The useless or irrelevant parts of the sentence are eradicated, so the crucial core meaning can be remembered.(economy of expression)','This answer has the clearest connection to our conclusion language, preferred = preferable.\n\nWhen we''re doing Strengthen + Principle, our biggest shortcut is to only consider answers (at least initially) that give us wording that match up with our conclusion.\n\nIn order to use this rule to weigh in on the Oral vs. Written head-to-head, we need to think\nOral traditions = economy of expression\nWritten traditions = verbosity\n\nThis is what the 2nd advantage of oral traditions was getting at. Oral traditions trim the fluff -- they eradicate the useless and irrelevant.\n\nWhile reading the paragraph, I interpreted these last two sentences to mean that in literate societies, there is so much writing that you fill up libraries and the reader doesn''t know what to read / what to believe / whom to trust. Meanwhile, in oral societies, they can only remember their top 100 stories / tales / instructional metaphors... so it''s sort of a survival of the fittest ideas.\n\nBut what this answer choice makes me think LSAC was really intending with the last two sentences is this:\n- Writing has no limits, so I can keep adding clauses and semicolons and modifiers and such until I have such a long, dense, poorly written sentence that the writer and reader are confused on the ultimate meaning of that sentence. (verbosity)\n\n- Oral traditions would make it really hard to remember a super long, complicated sentence. The useless or irrelevant parts of the sentence are eradicated, so the crucial core meaning can be remembered. (economy of expression)'),\n('993d0888-5658-4d59-b4c9-5fc2bd7103ee'::uuid,'We need a rule that takes what we''ve been told about Oral vs. Written traditions and allows to argue that Oral traditions arepreferable.\n\nNeither one of those traditions was described as containing \"ideas thatcannotbe discussed clearly\".\n\nAnd even if they had been, this rule would still just be saying that within either type of tradition, we shouldn''t discuss anything that can''t be discussed clearly. 102	('a50fc229-f56d-4335-9b73-0ad6407ce44e'::uuid,'This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies it is not in the town''s best interest to use the land for recreation. This is a different type of flaw — it assumes that choosing one use for land automatically means an alternative use is not in the town''s interest. The flaw here is about conflating a decision with a judgment about the best interest, or about opportunity cost and land use exclusivity. The stimulus''s flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from "not-X" to "opposite of X" — it draws an inference about competing uses of a resource. The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer treats two land uses as mutually exclusive and draws an evaluative conclusion about one from the selection of the other.','This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies that developing the southern, eastern, or western borders would be "equally acceptable," and it concludes that at least one of those other borders may also be developed. Whatever its weakness, it is a different type of reasoning: it moves from one decision to what is acceptable, and then possible, for similar decisions. The stimulus''s flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from "not-X" to "opposite of X." The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer never sorts anything into an opposite category.'),
103	('940c80cf-6110-4109-a232-3d85d374e963'::uuid,'This is about conflating a land-use decision with a judgment about best interests — a different flaw from the false dichotomy between friend and enemy in the stimulus.','This moves from developing the northern border to the other borders being "equally acceptable," and then to one of them possibly being developed. That is a different pattern from the false dichotomy between friend and enemy in the stimulus.'),
104	('a9b79f92-5188-48ec-81bd-dfb71aef277f'::uuid,'"We chose to build houses on the land, so a park must not be in our interest." That is not the same mistake as "Devan is not my friend, so he is my enemy." The first is about confusing a practical decision with a value judgment — you can think parks are great and still choose to build houses. The second is about leaping from one extreme to the opposite. Different logical errors, different aisles in the flaw supermarket.','"We are developing the north side, so developing the other sides is equally acceptable, so one of them might get developed too." That is not the same mistake as "Devan is not my friend, so he is my enemy." The first reasons from one decision to what is acceptable and possible elsewhere. The second leaps from one extreme to the opposite. Different logical errors, different aisles in the flaw supermarket.'),
105	('ddedb850-2b46-46e6-8f9e-c03f4c054790'::uuid,'This argument has a different logical structure from the stimulus. It establishes requirements for being a club officer (two years'' standing OR committee member) and concludes that someone who meets neither requirement is not an officer. This is actually valid reasoning — if the requirements are genuinely necessary conditions, failing all of them does disqualify someone. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from "fails friendship criteria" to "is an enemy" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer, which is the direct logical consequence of failing the criteria. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. It concludes "not-X" from failing the criteria for X, whereas the stimulus concluded "anti-X" from failing the criteria for X.','This argument has a different logical structure from the stimulus. It lists three ways to qualify as a club officer (two years'' standing OR committee member OR special qualifications) and concludes that Evelyn cannot be an officer because Evelyn fails the first two. That is flawed, but in a different way: it ignores the third route, since Evelyn might have special qualifications. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from "fails friendship criteria" to "is an enemy" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. It concludes "not-X" from failing some of the routes to X, whereas the stimulus concluded "anti-X" from failing the criteria for X.'),
106	('f941138e-1964-4565-a411-eb0fa41aefab'::uuid,'This is actually valid: failing the requirements for being an officer means you are not an officer. The stimulus''s flaw was leaping from "not a friend" to "an enemy." Here, the conclusion is simply "not an officer" — no false dichotomy.','This has a different flaw: there are three ways to become an officer, and the argument rules Evelyn out after checking only two. Evelyn might have special qualifications. The stimulus''s flaw was leaping from "not a friend" to "an enemy." Here, the conclusion is simply "not an officer" — no false dichotomy.'),
107	('3f80abcf-136e-4a99-8c28-61ff4a52bf6f'::uuid,'This argument says: "Does not meet the requirements for club officer, so not a club officer." That is just... correct. This answer just concludes "not qualified for A, so not A." No absurd leap. No false dichotomy. Just basic logic working as intended.','This argument says: "Fails two of the three routes to club officer, so not a club officer." That skips the third route, special qualifications, so it is flawed, just not the way the original is. The original argument said "does not meet friendship criteria, so ENEMY" — a massive logical leap from "not qualified for A" to "automatically in the worst possible category B." This answer only concludes "not A." No absurd leap. No false dichotomy. Just a different mistake: forgetting an option.'),
108	('5fea0e4e-46fc-44e6-9853-9e604ee3b872'::uuid,'This argument is logically valid, not flawed. It states that a plant needs sunny location AND regular watering to thrive. The plant is not in a sunny spot, so at least one necessary condition is unmet. The conclusion — that the plant will not thrive — follows logically. This is straightforward modus tollens: if both A and B are necessary for C, and A is absent, then C will not occur. The stimulus''s flaw was a false dichotomy — leaping from "not friend" to "enemy." This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that a necessary condition is unmet, therefore the outcome will not occur. There is no jump to an opposite extreme category.','This argument is logically sound, not flawed. It states that a plant needs a sunny location AND regular watering to thrive. The spot is not sunny and the plant was not watered regularly, so both necessary conditions are unmet. The conclusion, that this "explains why this plant is not as healthy as it should be," follows reasonably: if both A and B are necessary for C, and both are absent, C should not be expected. The stimulus''s flaw was a false dichotomy — leaping from "not friend" to "enemy." This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that unmet necessary conditions explain the poor outcome. There is no jump to an opposite extreme category.'),
109	('a764fa0d-651e-4019-bf65-843e99953034'::uuid,'This is valid reasoning: the plant needs sun and water to thrive, it lacks sun, so it will not thrive. No false dichotomy, no leap to an opposite category. Just sound modus tollens.','This is sound reasoning: the plant needs sun and regular water to thrive, it got neither, and that explains why it is not as healthy as it should be. No false dichotomy, no leap to an opposite category.'),
110	('bb281af7-f37f-46ab-a0a2-5873045820aa'::uuid,'Plant needs sun and water. Plant has no sun. Plant will not thrive. This is just... correct reasoning. There is no flaw to parallel because there is no flaw. It would be like trying to find a car accident that matches another car accident, and someone points at a car driving perfectly safely through a green light. Cannot match a flaw that does not exist.','Plant needs sun and water. Plant got no sun and no regular water. That explains why it is not doing well. This is just... correct reasoning. There is no flaw to parallel because there is no flaw. It would be like trying to find a car accident that matches another car accident, and someone points at a car driving perfectly safely through a green light. Cannot match a flaw that does not exist.'),
111	('e4b87a42-1013-4f48-b7a9-4f263d4945a7'::uuid,'If most students who take only single-discipline courses can effectively combine knowledge from different fields, that directly undermines the need for interdisciplinary courses. The educator''s argument depends on the idea that single-discipline instruction leaves students unable to integrate across fields. But this answer says students handle the integration just fine on their own. If students already possess the cross-disciplinary competence the educator wants to build, then restructuring the courses is unnecessary. This answer removes the very problem the educator''s proposal is designed to solve, which weakens rather than strengthens the conclusion.','If most students who take only single-discipline courses can effectively solve many problems faced in daily life, that undermines the need for interdisciplinary courses. The educator''s argument depends on the idea that single-discipline instruction leaves students poorly equipped for daily-life problems, which call for more than one field. But this answer says those students already handle many of those problems. If students already have the problem-solving ability the educator wants to build, then restructuring the courses looks unnecessary. This answer chips away at the very problem the educator''s proposal is designed to solve, which weakens rather than strengthens the conclusion.'),
112	('41c438b3-e939-4d46-b69f-fbb7e24daffe'::uuid,'If students already combine knowledge effectively from single-discipline courses, there is no need to switch to interdisciplinary courses. This removes the problem the educator''s proposal is trying to fix.','If most students in single-discipline courses can already effectively solve many daily-life problems, the case for switching to interdisciplinary courses weakens. This chips away at the problem the educator''s proposal is trying to fix.'),
113	('232001ae-8a83-49c8-b9ba-97b002a328a6'::uuid,'This answer basically says "students are already doing fine — they take separate courses and figure out how to put the knowledge together." They are already cooking the meal with single-discipline ingredients. This is the opposite of what the educator needs to hear.'),
114	('0202d8c0-b410-4b0e-aa2d-42044621f900'::uuid,'If common sense alone can solve most daily-life problems, this actually undermines the entire premise of the educator''s argument. The educator''s reasoning depends on the idea that daily-life problems require multi-disciplinary academic knowledge. If common sense handles most of those problems, then the need for any particular course structure — single-discipline or interdisciplinary — becomes questionable. But this answer does not strengthen the argument; if anything, it makes the entire debate about course structure less relevant. The educator''s recommendation is motivated by the difficulty of daily problems; if those problems are not actually that hard, the motivation evaporates regardless of whether you restructure the courses.','If common sense alone can solve most daily-life problems, that does not help the educator, and it does not contradict the premise either. The premise is a comparison: knowledge from a single discipline is rarely the most effective tool. Common sense solving problems "effectively" says nothing about which kind of course prepares students better. The answer cuts the same way against single-discipline and interdisciplinary courses, so it gives no reason to prefer the educator''s recommendation. It does not strengthen the argument.'),
115	('c920cae4-ea33-41bf-99fb-12e227b429b9'::uuid,'If common sense solves most daily problems, the educator''s entire premise weakens. 116	('8991fc0d-ed9b-4ce9-bb9c-0256e0ed8624'::uuid,'"Just use common sense" is the argument-killer here, not the argument-strengthener. 130	('60905f3a-fc49-4f33-97a6-d0c3dc1b103b'::uuid,'This supports the premise that players often control the movements of detailed human figures.','This tells us newer games do not use simple icons, which at most backs up the premise that players often control detailed human figures. 131	('114e83fd-ab48-4085-a287-8e874670d1ac'::uuid,'This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work, then the general rule holds because scholars cannot pursue their research without resources. The key phrase is "typically" — Plato and Aristotle were exceptions. Plato came from one of Athens'' wealthiest families, and Aristotle was supported by powerful patrons (eventually including Philip of Macedon). Their personal wealth and patronage meant they did not depend on the kind of institutional or governmental funding that political instability would have disrupted. The general rule remains valid: instability normally defunds intellectual work. The counterexample is explained: Plato and Aristotle had independent financial means that insulated them from the usual economic consequences of political turmoil. Both facts coexist perfectly.','This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work, then the general rule holds because thinkers cannot pursue their work without resources. The key word is "typically," and ancient Athens was the exception: there, wealthy citizens provided that support even during the unrest. So the usual reason instability blocks intellectual progress did not apply to Plato and Aristotle. The general rule remains valid: instability normally defunds intellectual work. The counterexample is explained: in Athens, private money from wealthy citizens filled the gap. Both facts coexist.'),
132	('f76218c3-1d77-47c4-af4e-9d4ede9da4d4'::uuid,'If instability normally blocks intellectual work by cutting off financial support, Plato and Aristotle are explained as exceptions who had independent wealth. The general rule holds (instability defunds scholarship), and the counterexample is resolved (they had personal resources).','If instability normally blocks intellectual work by cutting off financial support, Athens is explained as an exception: wealthy citizens provided that support anyway. The general rule holds (instability usually defunds intellectual work), and the counterexample is resolved (Athens had private backers).'),
133	('4c0009fb-f2c9-4d92-b760-afa4a8affb61'::uuid,'The general rule says instability kills intellectual progress. This answer reveals the murder weapon: money. Political chaos typically drains the funding that scholars need to, well, scholar. But Plato and Aristotle were not exactly applying for government grants. Plato was born into one of Athens'' wealthiest families, and Aristotle had a Macedonian king bankrolling his research. They were the ancient equivalent of independently wealthy hobbyists who could keep working even when the economy tanked. So the rule still works — instability kills funding, and no funding means no intellectual progress. Plato and Aristotle just had their own money, making them bulletproof to the usual financial consequences of political turmoil. Mystery solved.','The general rule says instability kills intellectual progress. This answer reveals the murder weapon: money. Political chaos typically drains the funding that thinkers need to, well, think. But in ancient Athens, wealthy citizens kept paying for intellectual work anyway. So the rule still works: instability usually kills funding, and no funding means no intellectual progress. Athens just had rich citizens who kept the money flowing, which shielded its thinkers from the usual financial consequences of political turmoil. Mystery solved.'),
134	('25055e28-603e-41ba-a038-2e99a1c467d8'::uuid,'The argument does not presume that effectiveness should be measured solely by improvement rates. In fact, improvement rate is a reasonable metric for evaluating treatment effectiveness. The argument''s flaw is not the choice of metric but the failure to control for confounding variables within that metric. Even if we accept improvement rate as the right measure, the comparison is still flawed because it does not account for differences in the types of conditions treated by specialists versus generalists. This answer misidentifies the problem: the measurement itself is fine; the comparison methodology is what fails. Two practitioners can both be measured by improvement rate, and the comparison can still be meaningless if they are treating fundamentally different patient populations.','The argument does not make this presumption. It never mentions patients who showed any improvement at all; its evidence and its conclusion are both about major improvement. So it cannot be inferring something about major improvement from facts about any improvement. The argument''s real flaw is the failure to control for confounding variables: it does not account for differences in the types of conditions treated by specialists versus generalists. Two practitioners can show the same major-improvement rates, and the comparison can still be meaningless if they are treating fundamentally different patient populations.'),
135	('90139a90-8ba5-4b66-a692-d81783ce4d98'::uuid,'Improvement rate is a reasonable metric. The flaw is not the measurement but the failure to control for different patient populations, making the comparison misleading despite using a valid metric.','The argument never relies on data about any improvement; both its evidence and its conclusion concern major improvement. So it does not make this presumption. The real flaw is the failure to control for different patient populations.'),
136	('97c6e92f-ce23-458c-b173-8443c249b3d2'::uuid,'There is nothing wrong with using improvement rates to measure how well a treatment works — that is actually a pretty sensible approach. The problem is not the ruler; it is that the argument measured two completely different things and declared them equal. Imagine weighing a bowling ball and a watermelon and, finding they weigh the same, declaring them identical objects. The scale is fine. The comparison is the problem.','This answer accuses the argument of reasoning from "any improvement" to "major improvement." But look at the argument: every number in it is about major improvement, and so is the conclusion. It never mentions any improvement at all. You cannot be accused of a leap you never took. The real problem is that the argument compared two practitioners without checking whether they treat the same kinds of patients.'),
137	('4078470b-e989-4112-a78d-0fb9d5f62cc4'::uuid,'The passage states that only primates have opposable thumbs — meaning ALL primates have them, and nothing else does. This means lemurs, as primates, have opposable thumbs. The passage does not say or imply that any lemurs lack opposable thumbs. The "only primates" formulation establishes that opposable thumbs are exclusive to primates, but it does not address variation within the primate order. Since lemurs are primates, the passage actually implies they DO have opposable thumbs, making this answer contradict the passage rather than follow from it.','The passage states that only primates have opposable thumbs. That means anything with opposable thumbs is a primate; it does not mean every primate has them. So the passage leaves open whether some lemurs lack opposable thumbs, but it gives no reason to think any do. With no support either way, this answer does not have to be true.'),
138	('42d4ea96-9561-4b73-8259-4af70ce4b965'::uuid,'The passage says only primates have opposable thumbs. Since lemurs are primates, they have them. Nothing suggests any lemurs lack opposable thumbs.','The passage says only primates have opposable thumbs: if something has them, it is a primate. That does not mean every primate has them, but nothing in the passage suggests any lemurs lack them either. This answer is not supported.'),
139	('4c57265c-53f7-4d19-9303-adf6bfe5111d'::uuid,'The passage says "only primates have opposable thumbs" — which means if you are a primate, you have them. Lemurs are primates. Therefore, lemurs have opposable thumbs. This answer tries to claim some lemurs are missing them, which goes against the grain of everything the passage said. It is like being told "only birds have feathers" and concluding "some birds do not have feathers." The passage gave lemurs their opposable thumbs by including them in the primate club. This answer tries to take those thumbs away without any textual support.','"Only primates have opposable thumbs" means if you have them, you are a primate. It does not mean every primate has them. Think of "only members can use the pool": that does not mean every member swims. So the passage does not promise lemurs their thumbs, but it does not take them away either. Nothing says any lemur lacks them, so this answer has no support and does not have to be true.'),
140	('e667bfa1-7239-4f50-87cf-0a48170a4d22'::uuid,'The passage says all higher primates are thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates. This tells us the ancestral species was diurnal, but it does not tell us that all current higher primates are diurnal. Evolution from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee that all descendants remain diurnal. Many higher primate species could have evolved nocturnal habits over millions of years. In fact, some higher primates (like certain prosimians reclassified or owl monkeys) are nocturnal. The passage provides no information about the current activity patterns of higher primates as a group — only about their evolutionary origin.','The passage says all higher primates are thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates. This tells us the ancestral species was diurnal, but it does not tell us that all current higher primates are diurnal. Evolution from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee that all descendants remain diurnal. Many higher primate species could have evolved nocturnal habits over millions of years. The passage provides no information about the current activity patterns of higher primates as a group — only about their evolutionary origin.'),
141	('38a47ea5-e7dc-41b3-9346-aa7cfd5955c0'::uuid,'The passage says some species of lemurs are the only living lower primates that are diurnal. This means some lemur species are diurnal, but it does not say all lemurs are diurnal. The word "some" leaves open the possibility that other lemur species are nocturnal. In fact, many lemur species in the real world are nocturnal, and the passage''s careful use of "some species of lemurs" rather than "all lemurs" signals that not every lemur is diurnal. This answer claims there are no nocturnal lemurs, which goes beyond — and likely contradicts — the passage''s careful "some" qualifier.','The passage says some species of lemurs are the only living lower primates that are diurnal. This means some lemur species are diurnal, but it does not say all lemurs are diurnal. The word "some" leaves open the possibility that other lemur species are nocturnal. This answer claims there are no nocturnal lemurs, which goes beyond what the passage states.'),
142	('23598e5b-a6c5-438e-b303-93c5b285bd3c'::uuid,'The passage says "some species of lemurs" are diurnal, not all. "Some" leaves room for other lemur species to be nocturnal. 143	('00dff09d-8234-48a2-81f7-057948292129'::uuid,'The passage said "SOME species of lemurs" are diurnal. SOME. Not all. Not every single one. Some. The flaw here is about conflating a decision with a judgment about the best interest, or about opportunity cost and land use exclusivity. The stimulus''s flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from \"not-X\" to \"opposite of X\" — it draws an inference about competing uses of a resource. The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer treats two land uses as mutually exclusive and draws an evaluative conclusion about one from the selection of the other.','This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies that developing the southern, eastern, or western borders would be \"equally acceptable,\" and it concludes that at least one of those other borders may also be developed. Whatever its weakness, it is a different type of reasoning: it moves from one decision to what is acceptable, and then possible, for similar decisions. The stimulus''s flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). It establishes requirements for being a club officer (two years'' standing OR committee member) and concludes that someone who meets neither requirement is not an officer. This is actually valid reasoning — if the requirements are genuinely necessary conditions, failing all of them does disqualify someone. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from \"fails friendship criteria\" to \"is an enemy\" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer, which is the direct logical consequence of failing the criteria. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from \"fails friendship criteria\" to \"is an enemy\" — a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. The stimulus''s flaw was a false dichotomy — leaping from \"not friend\" to \"enemy.\" This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that a necessary condition is unmet, therefore the outcome will not occur. This answer removes the very problem the educator''s proposal is designed to solve, which weakens rather than strengthens the conclusion.','If most students who take only single-discipline courses can effectively solve many problems faced in daily life, that undermines the need for interdisciplinary courses. This is the opposite of what the educator needs to hear.','This answer basically says \"students are already doing fine: they take separate courses and can still effectively solve many problems faced in daily life.\" If that is true, the educator''s argument takes a real hit. This is the opposite of what the educator needs to hear.'),\n('0202d8c0-b410-4b0e-aa2d-42044621f900'::uuid,'If common sense alone can solve most daily-life problems, this actually undermines the entire premise of the educator''s argument. The educator''s reasoning depends on the idea that daily-life problems require multi-disciplinary academic knowledge. If common sense handles most of those problems, then the need for any particular course structure — single-discipline or interdisciplinary — becomes questionable. But this answer does not strengthen the argument; if anything, it makes the entire debate about course structure less relevant. The educator''s recommendation is motivated by the difficulty of daily problems; if those problems are not actually that hard, the motivation evaporates regardless of whether you restructure the courses.','If common sense alone can solve most daily-life problems, that does not help the educator, and it does not contradict the premise either. The premise is a comparison: knowledge from a single discipline is rarely the most effective tool. Common sense solving problems \"effectively\" says nothing about which kind of course prepares students better. The answer cuts the same way against single-discipline and interdisciplinary courses, so it gives no reason to prefer the educator''s recommendation. It does not strengthen the argument.'),\n('c920cae4-ea33-41bf-99fb-12e227b429b9'::uuid,'If common sense solves most daily problems, the educator''s entire premise weakens. It does nothing to connect difficulty identifying with a figure to a game being less compelling, and that is the gap the argument needs filled.'),\n('114e83fd-ab48-4085-a287-8e874670d1ac'::uuid,'This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work, then the general rule holds because scholars cannot pursue their research without resources. The key phrase is \"typically\" — Plato and Aristotle were exceptions. Plato came from one of Athens'' wealthiest families, and Aristotle was supported by powerful patrons (eventually including Philip of Macedon). Their personal wealth and patronage meant they did not depend on the kind of institutional or governmental funding that political instability would have disrupted. The general rule remains valid: instability normally defunds intellectual work. The counterexample is explained: Plato and Aristotle had independent financial means that insulated them from the usual economic consequences of political turmoil. Both facts coexist perfectly.','This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work"}
